# 22. One thing I would NOT do

I wouldn't make a universal filter called:

> **Listing Type**

with:

- Device
- Part
- Device for Parts
- Discussion

That's technically possible, but UX-wise it's weaker.

Instead:

```text
Laptops

[ Complete Devices ] [ Parts ] [ For Parts ] [ Discussions ]
```

Each is effectively its own browsing experience.

The buyer immediately understands what they're looking at.

---

# 23. But there should still be a universal search

Even though we're focusing on browsing:

At the top:

> **Search Parts & Parcel**

The buyer could search:

> HP EliteBook

and get:

243 results

### Parts
181 results

56 results

### Discussions
32 results

That's another place where the four concepts work beautifully together.

---

# 24. So I see the Parts & Parcel home experience like this

```text
                         PARTS & PARCEL
                              │
                    What are you looking for?
                              │
          ┌───────────────────┼───────────────────┐
          │                   │                   │
          ▼                   ▼                   ▼
    Complete Devices        Parts             For Parts
          │                   │                   │
       Browse              Browse             Browse
          │                   │                   │
          └───────────────────┼───────────────────┘
                              │
                              ▼
                         Discussions
                              │
                       Ask the community
                              │
                              ▼
                       Find an offer
```

And underneath all of this:

```text
CATALOG
   │
   ├── Devices
   └── Parts
        │
        ▼
SELLER INVENTORY
        │
        ▼
LISTINGS
        │
        ▼
BUYERS
        │
        ▼
ORDERS
```

---

# 25. The most important UX principle

I think this is the principle we should carry through the entire redesign:

> **The catalog determines what the buyer can browse. The seller's inventory determines what can actually be sold. The listing determines how it's currently being sold.**

That means:

### Catalog

> HP EliteBook 840 G5 has a battery, screen, RAM, motherboard, etc.

### Adam's inventory

> 3 complete EliteBooks.

### Abel's inventory

> Several individual damaged/partial EliteBooks and their available components.

### Seth's inventory

> 10 batteries.

### Listings

The marketplace representations of those things.

And the buyer doesn't need to understand any of that.

---

## One final UX decision I would make

I would **not have one generic “Products” page** where complete laptops, batteries, donor laptops and discussions are all mixed together.

Instead, I'd make the user's mental model:

**“What am I trying to accomplish?”**

> **Buy a complete device** → Complete Devices

> **Find something to repair my device** → Parts

> **Buy a damaged/cheap device or salvage its components** → For Parts

> **Can't find it / need advice / want someone to identify something** → Discussions

That is a very natural marketplace experience.

And it gives Parts & Parcel something that ordinary e-commerce sites don't have: **the ability to browse an entire ecosystem around a physical product, not just a catalogue of finished products.**

The next thing I'd recommend is that we now take **one actual category—Laptops—and design the complete information architecture**: the category page, brand page, model page, the four browse tabs, filters, listing cards, details pages, and how a buyer moves between **Complete Devices ↔ Parts ↔ For Parts ↔ Discussions**. Once we have that, we can test it against Adam, Abel and Seth and see where the model breaks before touching the database.

---

### Message 16: **USER**

I suggest we take **Abel's four laptops and walk each one through its entire lifecycle**, including:

**listing → buyer sees it → buyer makes an offer → seller accepts → reservation → payment → sale → component separation → new listings → another sale → remaining components → final closure.**

While doing that, we'll explicitly identify **every state change and every relationship**.
That exercise will tell us whether our six concepts—**Catalog, Asset, Component, Inventory, Offer, Order**—are actually sufficient, or whether we've missed another important domain object before we touch the database.

---

### Message 17: **ASSISTANT (ChatGPT)**

Yes. And I want to do this exercise **before we write another migration**, because Abel's four laptops will expose the difficult cases much better than Adam or Seth.

There is also one thing I want to correct from our previous discussion:

> We used **Offer** to mean the seller's listing/offer for sale. But now we're talking about a **buyer making an offer**.

Those are two different concepts and **we should not use the same domain object for both**.

For the rest of this exercise, I'll use:

- **Listing** = seller's public offer for sale
- **Buyer Offer** = buyer proposes a price/terms
- **Reservation** = temporary hold
- **Order** = completed purchase transaction
- **Asset** = physical device
- **Component** = physical part belonging to an asset
- **Inventory** = seller's ownership/availability of physical things

That distinction is already one likely improvement to our model.

---

# 1. First, let's establish Abel's four physical laptops

We'll give them permanent IDs.

```text
ABEL
│
├── B1 — Laptop 1
│      Screen faulty
│      Seller wants to sell whole
│
├── B2 — Laptop 2
│      Motherboard faulty
│      Seller wants to sell parts individually
│
├── B3 — Laptop 3
│      Keyboard faulty
│      Parts listed individually
│
└── B4 — Laptop 4
       Screen faulty
       Initially listed whole
       Later motherboard sold separately
```

The critical thing is:

**B1, B2, B3 and B4 never cease to be the same physical assets.**

Their **selling state** changes.

Their **components' states** change.

Their **listings** change.

Their **orders** change.

But the physical identity remains traceable.

---

# 2. Before we start: the lifecycle vocabulary

This will make the rest much easier.

## Asset

A physical laptop:

> B4

## Component

A physical part of B4:

> B4-Motherboard

## Inventory

Abel owns B4 and its components.

## Listing

Abel tells the marketplace:

> "I am selling B4 for ₦150,000."

or:

> "I am selling B4's motherboard for ₦70,000."

## Buyer Offer

Buyer says:

> "I'll pay ₦60,000 for the motherboard."

## Reservation

The system temporarily holds the thing for that buyer.

## Order

The transaction has been accepted and is being fulfilled/completed.

## Sale

The ownership/availability of the physical thing changes as a result of the completed transaction.

---

# 3. Laptop B1 — Sell the whole laptop

This is our simplest lifecycle.

---

## STATE 1 — Abel acquires B1

```text
Asset B1
│
├── Device: HP EliteBook 840 G5
├── Owner: Abel
└── Status: Available
```

Its components exist conceptually:

```text
B1
├── Screen
├── Motherboard
├── RAM
├── Storage
├── Battery
├── Keyboard
└── Casing
```

Screen is faulty.

So:

```text
B1
│
├── Screen → Faulty
├── Motherboard → Working/Unknown
├── RAM → Working/Unknown
├── Storage → Working/Unknown
└── ...
```

---

# 4. B1 — Abel creates the listing

Abel chooses:

> List an item → Device → HP EliteBook 840 G5

He says:

> Screen is damaged.

Then:

> **Sell as complete device**

He enters:

> ₦150,000

The system creates:

```text
LISTING L1

Seller: Abel
Asset: B1
Type: Complete Device
Price: ₦150,000
Quantity: 1
Status: Active
```

Notice:

### Listing ≠ Asset

The listing is merely Abel's **current intention to sell B1**.

---

# 5. B1 — Buyer sees it

Buyer browses:

**Laptops → Complete Devices**

They see:

> HP EliteBook 840 G5  
> Screen damaged  
> ₦150,000  
> 1 available

The buyer opens L1.

The details page is backed by:

```text
Listing L1
     │
     ▼
Asset B1
     │
     ├── Screen: Faulty
     ├── Motherboard
     ├── RAM
     └── ...
```

The buyer does **not** see B1 as an internal ID.

They see:

> This specific laptop.

---

# 6. B1 — Buyer makes an offer

Buyer says:

> "I'll pay ₦120,000."

Now we need a new object:

```text
BUYER OFFER BO1
│
├── Buyer: Customer X
├── Listing: L1
├── Proposed price: ₦120,000
└── Status: Pending
```

This is **not** the same as Listing L1.

So:

```text
Listing
    │
    └── Buyer Offers
          ├── BO1
          ├── BO2
          └── ...
```

This is one of the important things our earlier six concepts did not capture.

---

# 7. B1 — Abel accepts

Abel accepts ₦120,000.

BO1:

```text
Pending
   ↓
Accepted
```

But this **doesn't mean the sale is completed yet**.

That's important.

We still need:

> Reservation → payment → order → fulfillment → completion.

---

# 8. B1 — Reservation

The system creates:

```text
RESERVATION R1

Asset: B1
Buyer: Customer X
Listing: L1
Expires: ...
Status: Active
```

And B1 changes:

```text
AVAILABLE
    ↓
RESERVED
```

The listing should now show:

> Reserved / temporarily unavailable

This prevents another buyer from buying B1 simultaneously.

---

# 9. B1 — Payment

Customer pays ₦120,000.

Payment becomes:

```text
PAYMENT P1
│
├── Amount: ₦120,000
├── Buyer: Customer X
├── Order: O1
└── Status: Successful
```

Now we need an **Order**.

---

# 10. B1 — Order

```text
ORDER O1
│
├── Buyer: Customer X
├── Seller: Abel
├── Listing: L1
├── Asset: B1
├── Price: ₦120,000
└── Status: Processing
```

This is the commercial transaction.

Then:

```text
B1
Reserved
   ↓
Sold / Fulfillment
```

After successful handover:

```text
B1
Available
   ↓
Reserved
   ↓
Sold
```

Abel no longer has B1 available for sale.

---

# 11. B1 — What happens to the listing?

L1:

```text
Active
 ↓
Reserved
 ↓
Sold
 ↓
Closed
```

The listing remains as historical information.

We don't delete it.

That's important for:

- order history
- seller history
- dispute resolution
- analytics
- auditing

---

# 12. B1 lifecycle summary

```text
ASSET B1
Available
   │
   ▼
Listed
   │
   ▼
Buyer Offer
   │
   ▼
Accepted
   │
   ▼
Reserved
   │
   ▼
Paid
   │
   ▼
Sold
   │
   ▼
No longer in Abel's available inventory
```

Nothing difficult here.

Now let's attack the difficult cases.

---

# 13. Laptop B2 — Sell individual parts

B2 has:

> Faulty motherboard.

Abel wants to sell:

- Screen
- HDD/SSD
- RAM

individually.

---

## B2 starts as one asset

```text
B2
│
├── Screen
├── Motherboard → Faulty
├── RAM
├── Storage
├── Battery
└── Keyboard
```

Initially:

```text
Asset B2
Status: Available
Selling strategy: Parts
```

But I would **not store "selling strategy" as a permanent property of the asset**.

Why?

Because tomorrow Abel may change his mind.

Instead, it is represented by the listings.

---

# 14. Abel creates three listings

### Listing L2A

> HP EliteBook 840 G5 Screen  
> ₦100,000

Backed by:

> B2-Screen

### Listing L2B

> HP EliteBook 840 G5 RAM  
> ₦40,000

Backed by:

> B2-RAM

### Listing L2C

> HP EliteBook 840 G5 Storage  
> ₦35,000

Backed by:

> B2-Storage

So:

```text
                 B2
                  │
        ┌─────────┼─────────┐
        ▼         ▼         ▼
      Screen      RAM     Storage
        │         │          │
       L2A       L2B        L2C
```

This is a very important relationship:

> **One asset can have multiple listings through its components.**

---

# 15. Buyer purchases B2's RAM

Buyer sees:

> HP EliteBook 840 G5 RAM — ₦40,000

They buy it.

Now:

```text
B2-RAM
Available
   ↓
Reserved
   ↓
Sold
```

Listing L2B:

```text
Active
   ↓
Sold
```

Order O2:

```text
Buyer → Abel
Item → B2-RAM
Amount → ₦40,000
```

But B2 itself is **not sold**.

This is critical.

```text
B2
│
├── Screen → Available
├── Motherboard → Faulty
├── RAM → SOLD
├── Storage → Available
├── Battery → Available
└── Keyboard → Available
```

Therefore:

> **Selling a component does not sell the parent asset.**

---

# 16. Buyer purchases B2's screen

Same process.

```text
Screen
Available
   ↓
Reserved
   ↓
Sold
```

Now:

```text
B2
│
├── Screen → SOLD
├── Motherboard → Faulty
├── RAM → SOLD
├── Storage → Available
├── Battery → Available
└── Keyboard → Available
```

B2 is still physically present.

But its state has changed.

---

# 17. What should B2's overall asset status be?

This is where I think we should **not** use just:

> Available / Sold

because that's too simplistic.

B2 is now:

> **Partially dismantled / Partially sold**

or perhaps internally:

> **Partially allocated**

The user-facing wording could be:

> **Parts remaining**

We can later decide the exact state names.

The important thing is that the system needs to know:

> **The parent asset still exists, but some of its components have been sold.**

---

# 18. B2 — Abel changes his mind

Suppose he decides:

> "I'll sell the remaining parts together."

Remaining:

```text
Storage
Battery
Keyboard
```

He creates:

### Listing L2D

> HP EliteBook 840 G5 — Remaining Parts  
> ₦80,000

This listing is backed by:

```text
B2
├── Storage
├── Battery
└── Keyboard
```

This is a **bundle listing**.

And this reveals another domain concept.

We need to represent:

> **A listing can contain one or more inventory items/components.**

That's more general than saying:

> listing belongs to asset

because:

- a whole-device listing → one asset
- a part listing → one component
- a bundle listing → multiple components

This will matter enormously in the database.

---

# 19. B2 — buyer buys the remaining bundle

Buyer purchases L2D.

The order contains:

```text
ORDER O3

Items:
├── B2-Storage
├── B2-Battery
└── B2-Keyboard
```

After completion:

```text
B2
│
├── Screen → SOLD
├── Motherboard → Faulty
├── RAM → SOLD
├── Storage → SOLD
├── Battery → SOLD
└── Keyboard → SOLD
```

Now there is essentially nothing useful left to sell.

B2 becomes:

> **Closed / Fully dismantled**

This is different from:

> Sold

because the **whole laptop was never sold as a laptop**.

The components were sold.

That's a subtle but important distinction.

---

# 20. B2 teaches us something important

We cannot model inventory simply as:

```text
seller
   ↓
product
   ↓
quantity
```

because B2's inventory is hierarchical:

```text
B2
│
├── Screen
├── Motherboard
├── RAM
├── Storage
├── Battery
└── Keyboard
```

And those child things can have **independent commercial lifecycles**.

---

# 21. Laptop B3 — the really interesting lifecycle

B3:

> Keyboard faulty.

Abel initially lists parts individually.

```text
B3
│
├── Screen → Listing
├── Motherboard → Listing
├── RAM → Listing
├── Storage → Listing
├── Battery → Listing
└── Keyboard → Faulty
```

Then someone buys the screen.

---

# 22. B3 — screen is sold

```text
B3-Screen
Available
   ↓
Reserved
   ↓
Sold
```

Now Abel decides:

> "I want to sell everything remaining as one package."

This is where we need to be careful.

He is not creating a new physical asset.

He is creating a **new listing containing multiple existing inventory items**.

So:

```text
B3
│
├── Screen → SOLD
├── Motherboard → Available
├── RAM → Available
├── Storage → Available
├── Battery → Available
└── Keyboard → Faulty
```

New listing:

```text
L3B

"HP EliteBook 840 G5 — Remaining Parts"

Contains:
├── Motherboard
├── RAM
├── Storage
└── Battery
```

Keyboard is excluded because it is faulty.

---

# 23. Now suppose someone buys L3B

Order O4 contains:

```text
B3-Motherboard
B3-RAM
B3-Storage
B3-Battery
```

After completion:

```text
B3
│
├── Screen → SOLD
├── Motherboard → SOLD
├── RAM → SOLD
├── Storage → SOLD
├── Battery → SOLD
└── Keyboard → Faulty
```

The asset is now:

> **Closed**

But notice:

**The faulty keyboard still exists physically.**

This matters.

We shouldn't pretend it disappeared.

It could potentially be:

- discarded
- retained by Abel
- recycled
- sold later
- listed as a faulty component

Therefore:

> **Asset closed does not necessarily mean every physical component has disappeared.**

It means:

> The asset is no longer an active sellable assembly.

---

# 24. B4 — this is the most important scenario

Initially:

> Screen faulty.

Abel wants to sell whole laptop.

```text
B4
│
├── Screen → Faulty
├── Motherboard → Available
├── RAM → Available
├── Storage → Available
├── Battery → Available
└── Keyboard → Available
```

Listing:

```text
L4

HP EliteBook 840 G5
Screen damaged
₦150,000
Complete device
```

---

# 25. Buyer doesn't want the whole device

Buyer says:

> "I'll give you ₦70,000 for the motherboard."

This is **not automatically a new listing**.

It's a buyer offer against an existing listing.

So:

```text
Buyer Offer BO4
│
├── Buyer: Customer Y
├── Target: B4-Motherboard
├── Existing listing: L4
├── Amount: ₦70,000
└── Status: Pending
```

But here's a question:

### Was B4-Motherboard actually listed separately?

No.

So our system needs to allow a buyer to make an offer for a **subset of what is being sold**.

This is another reason why we need to separate:

> **Asset/component**

from:

> **Listing**

---

# 26. Abel accepts the motherboard offer

Now we have a problem to solve.

The original listing says:

> Sell complete B4.

But Abel has accepted:

> Sell motherboard only.

Therefore L4 cannot remain active in its original form.

The system needs to transition it.

I would do:

```text
L4
Complete Device — ₦150k
        │
        ▼
    Superseded
```

And create a transaction/reservation for:

> B4-Motherboard

Then:

```text
B4-Motherboard
Available
   ↓
Reserved
```

The rest remains available internally.

---

# 27. Payment and order

Buyer pays ₦70,000.

Order O5:

```text
ORDER O5
│
├── Buyer: Customer Y
├── Seller: Abel
├── Item: B4-Motherboard
├── Amount: ₦70,000
└── Status: Processing
```

After fulfillment:

```text
B4-Motherboard
Reserved
   ↓
Sold
```

Now:

```text
B4
│
├── Screen → Faulty
├── Motherboard → SOLD
├── RAM → Available
├── Storage → Available
├── Battery → Available
└── Keyboard → Available
```

---

# 28. What happens to the original B4 listing?

This is important.

It should not remain:

> "HP EliteBook — ₦150k"

because the motherboard is gone.

We have several choices.

I think the best behavior is:

### Original listing:

> **Closed / Superseded**

Then Abel is prompted:

> **Your laptop has changed. Create a new listing for the remaining parts?**

He clicks:

> Yes.

---

# 29. Abel chooses "Sell remaining parts individually"

The system knows:

```text
B4
├── Screen → Faulty
├── Motherboard → SOLD
├── RAM → Available
├── Storage → Available
├── Battery → Available
└── Keyboard → Available
```

Abel chooses:

> **List individually**

The system proposes:

```text
RAM
₦40,000

Storage
₦35,000

Battery
₦25,000

Keyboard
₦30,000
```

He can edit prices.

Then creates:

```text
L4A → RAM
L4B → Storage
L4C → Battery
L4D → Keyboard
```

Now B4 has transformed from:

```text
Complete Device listing
```

into:

```text
Multiple Part listings
```

---

# 30. Or Abel could choose a bundle

Instead:

> **Sell remaining parts together**

The system generates:

```text
L4E

HP EliteBook 840 G5
Remaining Parts

Includes:
✓ RAM
✓ Storage
✓ Battery
✓ Keyboard

Missing:
✗ Motherboard
✗ Screen

Price: ₦100,000
```

That's a valid listing.

---

# 31. Or Abel can do both

This is an interesting question.

Could he list:

> RAM — ₦40k

and simultaneously:

> RAM + Storage + Battery + Keyboard — ₦100k

?

**Yes, potentially.**

But then we have an inventory reservation problem.

If someone buys the RAM individually, the bundle becomes invalid.

So the system needs **listing dependencies**.

For example:

```text
Bundle Listing L4E
        │
        ├── RAM
        ├── Storage
        ├── Battery
        └── Keyboard

Individual Listing L4A
        │
        └── RAM
```

If RAM sells:

```text
L4A → Sold

L4E → Automatically unavailable
```

This is a significant domain rule.

---

# 32. This is telling us something about Listing

A listing isn't merely:

```text
product_id
price
quantity
```

It needs to identify **what inventory it is offering**.

Conceptually:

```text
LISTING
│
├── seller
├── price
├── status
├── visibility
├── terms
│
└── listing items
      │
      ├── asset
      ├── component
      └── perhaps inventory stock
```

And a listing can have:

### One whole asset

```text
L1
└── B1
```

### One component

```text
L2A
└── B2-Screen
```

### Multiple components

```text
L3B
├── B3-Motherboard
├── B3-RAM
├── B3-Storage
└── B3-Battery
```

That's a much more powerful model.

---

# 33. Let's now look at all four side by side

## B1 — Whole device

```text
B1
 │
 └── L1
       │
       └── Order
             │
             └── SOLD
```

---

## B2 — Parts individually

```text
B2
│
├── Screen ───── L2A ─── Order ─── SOLD
│
├── RAM ──────── L2B ─── Order ─── SOLD
│
├── Storage ───── L2C ─── Order ─── SOLD
│
├── Battery ─────── available
│
└── Keyboard ─────── available
```

Then remaining components can become:

```text
B2
│
└── Remaining components
        │
        └── L2D (bundle)
                │
                └── Order
```

---

## B3 — Partially sold, then bundle

```text
B3
│
├── Screen ───── L3A ─── SOLD
│
├── Motherboard ────────┐
├── RAM ────────────────┤
├── Storage ────────────┤
└── Battery ────────────┘
                         │
                         ▼
                      L3B
                    (Bundle)
                         │
                         ▼
                       SOLD
```

---

## B4 — Whole listing converted after buyer offer

```text
B4
│
├── Original listing L4
│       │
│       └── Superseded
│
├── Motherboard
│       │
│       └── Buyer Offer
│              │
│              └── Order
│                   │
│                   └── SOLD
│
├── RAM ───────────── L4A
├── Storage ───────── L4B
├── Battery ───────── L4C
└── Keyboard ──────── L4D
```

That is the model we need.

---

# 34. Now let's identify every state change

This is where the exercise becomes useful for database design.

## Asset states

An asset can move through things like:

```text
Available
   ↓
Listed
   ↓
Reserved
   ↓
Sold
```

But for Abel we also need:

```text
Available
   ↓
Partially dismantled
   ↓
Partially sold
   ↓
Closed
```

Potentially:

```text
Available
   ↓
Listed as whole
   ↓
Converted to parts
```

So I would **not make "Listed" an asset status**.

Why?

Because an asset can have multiple listings.

Instead:

### Asset status describes the physical asset.

### Listing status describes the marketplace listing.

That separation is very important.

---

# 35. Listing states

A listing might have:

```text
Draft
Active
Paused
Reserved
Partially sold
Sold
Expired
Cancelled
Superseded
Closed
```

But we shouldn't blindly implement all of these yet.

The important distinction is:

> **Listing state ≠ inventory state ≠ asset state.**

For example:

B4:

```text
Asset:
Partially sold

Original listing:
Superseded

Motherboard:
Sold

RAM:
Available

New RAM listing:
Active
```

All of those states can coexist.

---

# 36. Component states

A component can be:

```text
Present
Available
Listed
Reserved
Sold
Faulty
Discarded
Removed
```

But again, we need to be careful.

"Listed" isn't really a physical state.

So internally I'd probably separate:

### Physical state

```text
Present
Removed
Faulty
Discarded
```

from:

### Commercial availability

```text
Available
Reserved
Sold
```

That is cleaner.

---

# 37. Inventory states

Inventory is really telling us:

> What can Abel currently sell?

So:

```text
Available
Reserved
Unavailable
Sold
```

But the inventory record may refer to:

- whole asset
- component
- quantity stock

This is why I am beginning to think **Inventory should not necessarily be a physical object itself**.

It may be better represented as a **stock/availability layer** around physical items.

We'll investigate that when we get to the schema.

---

# 38. Order states

Order needs its own lifecycle:

```text
Pending Payment
Paid
Processing
Ready for Fulfillment
In Transit
Delivered
Completed
Cancelled
Disputed
Refunded
```

The exact states depend on your fulfillment system, but the important thing is:

> **Order is not the physical item's state.**

---

# 39. Buyer Offer states

A buyer offer needs:

```text
Pending
Accepted
Rejected
Withdrawn
Expired
Cancelled
Converted to Order
```

And here's an important rule:

### An accepted Buyer Offer is not necessarily an Order.

Acceptance means:

> Seller agreed to the proposed terms.

Then the transaction still needs to go through:

> reservation/payment/order.

Depending on your business rules, acceptance could create the reservation automatically.

---

# 40. Reservation is definitely a separate concept

I think this exercise makes that clear.

Suppose Abel accepts ₦120k.

Before payment:

```text
B1
Available
```

After acceptance:

```text
B1
Reserved for Buyer X
```

But if Buyer X doesn't pay within 30 minutes:

```text
Reservation expires
```

Then:

```text
B1
Reserved
   ↓
Available
```

The listing becomes available again.

Therefore Reservation has its own lifecycle.

---

# 41. Payment is also separate

A payment can fail while the reservation exists.

Example:

```text
Reservation
Active

Payment
Failed
```

Then buyer tries again.

Or:

```text
Payment
Successful

Order
Created
```

So Payment should not be embedded as a simple `paid = true` on the order.

We'll need a proper payment relationship, especially in Nigeria where you may have:

- card
- bank transfer
- USSD
- wallet
- payment gateway
- possibly escrow

---

# 42. So are the original six concepts sufficient?

**No.**

After walking Abel's four laptops through the lifecycle, I would now say our conceptual model needs at least these:

```text
1. Catalog
2. Asset
3. Component
4. Inventory / Stock
5. Listing
6. Buyer Offer
7. Reservation
8. Order
9. Payment
```

And possibly:

```text
10. Listing Item / Listing Contents
11. Bundle
12. Fulfillment
13. Discussion
```

But I don't want to declare all of those as independent database entities yet.

Some may be **relationships/value objects/state records**, rather than top-level domain objects.

---

# 43. There is another important thing we discovered

The old question:

> "Is this seller listing the device or listing its parts?"

is actually the wrong question.

The seller isn't fundamentally choosing a **listing type**.

They are choosing:

> **Which physical inventory do I want to make available for purchase, and how do I want to package it for sale?**

That allows:

### Whole device

```text
B4
```

### Individual part

```text
B4-Motherboard
```

### Multiple parts

```text
B4-RAM
B4-Storage
B4-Battery
```

### Multiple identical stock

```text
Seth's 10 batteries
```

All through the same marketplace mechanism.

---

# 44. This also solves the "device for parts" problem

I would now **not treat "For Parts" as a fundamentally different inventory type.**

It's a **buyer-facing classification of a listing**.

For example:

Abel lists B4:

> HP EliteBook 840 G5 — For Parts

Internally:

```text
Listing
   ↓
Asset B4
```

The asset itself is still:

> HP EliteBook 840 G5.

"For Parts" tells the buyer:

> **This listing represents a damaged/incomplete/non-functional device or a set of components intended for repair/salvage.**

That's a much cleaner model.

---

# 45. And "Parts" is also buyer-facing

Internally:

> Component

Buyer sees:

> Part

So:

```text
Internal:
Component

Marketplace:
Part
```

Likewise:

```text
Internal:
Listing

Marketplace:
Listing
```

Here the terminology is naturally understandable, so we can use it both internally and externally.

---

# 46. Discussions are slightly different

Your forum/community concept doesn't belong inside the asset lifecycle.

It is its own ecosystem.

But it can **reference catalog items, listings, devices, parts and sellers.**

For example:

> "I'm looking for HP EliteBook 840 G5 motherboard."

The discussion could be associated with:

```text
Catalog Model
     ↓
HP EliteBook 840 G5
```

Someone could reply:

> "I have one."

and potentially attach:

> a listing.

So:

```text
Discussion
   │
   ├── Catalog reference
   │
   ├── Replies
   │
   └── Listings / Offers
```

This is another reason I wouldn't make Discussions merely a product category.

---

# 47. The emerging architecture

At this point, I see the system roughly like this:

```text
                         CATALOG
                            │
              ┌─────────────┴─────────────┐
              │                           │
           DEVICE                        PART
              │                           │
           MODEL                    PART DEFINITION
              │                           │
              └─────────────┬─────────────┘
                            │
                            ▼
                       INVENTORY
                            │
              ┌─────────────┴─────────────┐
              │                           │
          WHOLE ASSET                 COMPONENT
              │                           │
              └─────────────┬─────────────┘
                            │
                            ▼
                         LISTING
                            │
                 ┌──────────┴──────────┐
                 │                     │
             Buyer Offer          Direct Purchase
                 │                     │
                 └──────────┬──────────┘
                            ▼
                       RESERVATION
                            │
                            ▼
                         PAYMENT
                            │
                            ▼
                          ORDER
                            │
                            ▼
                       FULFILLMENT
                            │
                            ▼
                          SALE
```

And alongside it:

```text
DISCUSSIONS
    │
    ├── Catalog
    ├── Parts
    ├── Listings
    ├── Sellers
    └── Buyer requests
```

---

# 48. The most important discovery

I think we've found the central architectural principle for Parts & Parcel:

> **The physical asset has a life of its own, independent of how the seller chooses to sell it.**

B4 is always B4.

Its journey might be:

```text
One physical laptop
       │
       ▼
Listed whole
       │
       ▼
Buyer wants motherboard
       │
       ▼
Motherboard sold
       │
       ▼
Remaining components
       │
       ├── RAM sold
       ├── Battery sold
       ├── Storage sold
       └── Keyboard sold
       │
       ▼
Asset closed
```

The marketplace listings come and go **around the physical asset**.

That is much more robust than making the listing itself the source of truth.

---

# 49. And this changes how I would design the database

I would **not** proceed directly from this to:

> `devices`, `parts`, `inventory`, `offers`, `orders`

because we now know that would probably reproduce the same problem in a cleaner-looking form.

Before migration, I think we should do **one more modeling exercise**:

### Model the actual relationships and ownership.

Specifically, we need to answer:

1. **What exactly is an Asset?**
2. **What exactly is a Component?**
3. Can a Component itself have sub-components?
4. How does an Asset become a collection of Components?
5. Can a component exist independently from an asset?
6. How does Seth's 10 identical batteries fit into the same inventory system as Abel's unique motherboard?
7. What exactly does a Listing contain?
8. Can a Listing contain both a whole Asset and Components?
9. How do bundle listings work?
10. How do competing listings share the same physical inventory?
11. What happens to a listing when one of its underlying components sells?
12. What exactly happens to inventory when a buyer makes an offer?
13. When does reservation occur?
14. When does ownership actually change?
15. Can the same physical asset ever return to available inventory after cancellation/return?
16. How do returns affect the asset/component history?

**Once we answer those, we will have a genuinely solid domain model.**

And I strongly recommend we do that **before Laravel migrations**, because the Abel B1–B4 exercise has already shown that the original `device → parts → listing` structure is not enough.

The good news is that I don't think we've made a mess—we've actually uncovered the right abstraction. The key is that **Parts & Parcel is not merely a marketplace for products; it is a marketplace that understands physical devices, their parts, and the different ways those physical things can be sold over their lifetime.**

---
