### Message 28: **ASSISTANT (ChatGPT)**

Yes. **I think your offer logic is better**, and it gives us a very clean mechanism.

If Abel sends the same offer to Buyer A and Buyer B:

```text
                Abel's Offer
                 ₦70,000
                     │
          ┌──────────┴──────────┐
          ▼                     ▼
      Buyer A               Buyer B
      Accepts               Accepts
       FIRST                SECOND
          │                     │
          ▼                     ▼
     RESERVED                PENDING
          │                     │
          │                "Acceptance
          │                 received"
          │                     │
          ▼                     │
       Payment                  │
          │                     │
     ┌────┴────┐                │
     │         │                │
   Pays      Expires            │
     │         │                │
     ▼         ▼                ▼
   SOLD     RELEASE ───────→ RESERVED
                              │
                              ▼
                           Payment
```

The second buyer **doesn't need to accept again**. Their acceptance is already recorded. It is simply waiting for the inventory to become available.

That is elegant.

---

# Now let's step back

You're absolutely right that we've accumulated too many concepts in our heads.

Before we touch migrations, UX screens, or state machines, **we need a map of the whole system.**

And I don't want to simply dump 30 model names on you.

Let's organize Parts & Parcel into **domains**, then show how those domains connect.

---

# Parts & Parcel — Domain Map

At the highest level, I currently see **9 domains**.

```text
                         PARTS & PARCEL
                                │
        ┌───────────┬───────────┼───────────┬───────────┐
        │           │           │           │           │
        ▼           ▼           ▼           ▼           ▼
     IDENTITY    CATALOG     INVENTORY   MARKETPLACE  COMMUNITY
        │           │           │           │           │
        │           │           │           │           │
        ▼           ▼           ▼           ▼           ▼
      USERS       DEVICES      ASSETS     LISTINGS    DISCUSSIONS
                  PARTS       COMPONENTS    OFFERS     RESPONSES
```

And underneath:

```text
                         PARTS & PARCEL
                                │
              ┌─────────────────┼─────────────────┐
              │                 │                 │
              ▼                 ▼                 ▼
         TRANSACTIONS       FULFILLMENT       FINANCE
              │                 │                 │
              ▼                 ▼                 ▼
           CARTS             SHIPMENTS         PAYMENTS
           OFFERS            PICKUPS            FEES
         RESERVATIONS        RETURNS            PAYOUTS
           ORDERS                               REFUNDS
              │
              ▼
          TRUST & SAFETY
              │
        DISPUTES / REVIEWS
```

And:

```text
                 USERS
                   │
          ┌────────┴────────┐
          ▼                 ▼
        BUYER             SELLER
          │                 │
          │                 ├── SUBSCRIPTION
          │                 ├── STORE
          │                 └── RESPONSE ALLOWANCE
          │
          └────────────┐
                       ▼
                  TRANSACTIONS
```

Let's now break this down properly.

---

# 1. IDENTITY DOMAIN

This answers:

> **Who is using Parts & Parcel?**

### Models

- **User**
- **Seller Profile**
- **Buyer Profile** — possibly unnecessary as a separate model initially
- **Address**
- **Verification**
- **User Role / Account capability**

The key idea is:

> A person doesn't permanently become a "seller."

The same user can be:

```text
Today:
Buyer

Tomorrow:
Seller

Next week:
Buyer + Seller
```

That's fundamental to our platform.

---

# 2. CATALOG DOMAIN

This answers:

> **What things exist in the Parts & Parcel universe?**

This is where your original idea of:

> Categories → Brand → Model → Device → Parts

gets refined.

### Models

- **Category**
- **Brand**
- **Model**
- **Component Type**

For example:

```text
Category
   │
   ▼
Laptop
   │
   ▼
HP
   │
   ▼
EliteBook 840 G5
```

And:

```text
EliteBook 840 G5
        │
        ├── Motherboard
        ├── Screen
        ├── Keyboard
        ├── RAM
        ├── SSD
        ├── Battery
        └── Charger
```

The Catalog describes **what a thing is**.

It does NOT describe who owns it or whether it's available.

---

# 3. INVENTORY / ASSET DOMAIN

This answers:

> **What physical things actually exist?**

This is one of the most important distinctions in our system.

### Models

- **Asset**
- **Component**
- **Inventory**
- Possibly **Inventory Unit**

Remember Abel.

He owns:

```text
B4 — physical HP EliteBook
       │
       ├── Screen
       ├── Motherboard
       ├── RAM
       ├── SSD
       ├── Battery
       └── Keyboard
```

The catalog says:

> "HP EliteBook 840 G5."

The asset says:

> **"This particular physical laptop belonging to Abel."**

The components say:

> **"These physical pieces belong to that laptop."**

---

# 4. MARKETPLACE DOMAIN

This answers:

> **How does an owner make something available for sale?**

### Models

- **Listing**
- **Listing Item**
- **Offer**
- **Reservation**
- **Cart**

This is where the physical inventory becomes something a buyer can interact with.

For example:

```text
ABEL'S ASSET
     │
     ▼
Component: Motherboard
     │
     ▼
LISTING
     │
     ├── Price
     ├── Condition
     ├── Photos
     └── Availability
```

And then:

```text
Buyer
  │
  ▼
Listing
  │
  ├── Add to Cart
  │
  ├── Buy
  │
  └── Make Offer
```

---

# 5. TRANSACTION DOMAIN

This answers:

> **How does an interested buyer become a customer?**

### Models

- **Cart**
- **Cart Item**
- **Offer**
- **Reservation**
- **Order**
- **Order Item**

There is some overlap with Marketplace because these boundaries aren't final yet.

For example, we may eventually decide:

> Offer belongs in Transactions rather than Marketplace.

That's fine.

We're mapping the domain conceptually first.

---

# 6. FULFILLMENT DOMAIN

This answers:

> **How does the physical thing get from seller to buyer?**

### Models

- **Fulfillment**
- **Shipment**
- **Pickup**
- **Delivery**
- **Return**
- **Return Shipment**

Potentially:

```text
ORDER
  │
  ▼
FULFILLMENT
  │
  ├── Shipment
  │      └── Tracking
  │
  └── Pickup
         └── Confirmation
```

This is where the earlier discussions about:

- pickup
- shipment
- delivery
- return
- replacement

fit.

---

# 7. FINANCE DOMAIN

This answers:

> **Where does the money go?**

### Models

- **Payment**
- **Payment Transaction**
- **Escrow / Protected Transaction**
- **Platform Fee**
- **Seller Payout**
- **Refund**

Conceptually:

```text
BUYER
  │
  ▼
PAYMENT
  │
  ▼
PROTECTED FUNDS
  │
  ├───────────────┐
  │               │
  ▼               ▼
SELLER PAYOUT   REFUND
  │
  ▼
PLATFORM FEE
```

The exact regulatory/payment-provider architecture will need to be designed separately from our domain model.

---

# 8. COMMUNITY DOMAIN

This is the part that makes Parts & Parcel different from a normal ecommerce store.

### Models

- **Discussion**
- **Response**
- **Response Usage**
- **Offer** — potentially linked to a response
- **Discussion Attachment**

Example:

```text
BUYER
  │
  ▼
Discussion

"Looking for HP EliteBook 840 G5 motherboard"
  │
  ├── Response — "I have one"
  │
  ├── Response — "Try Computer Village"
  │
  ├── Response — "I have one for ₦70k"
  │                         │
  │                         ▼
  │                       OFFER
  │
  └── Response — "Is it tested?"
```

And every response consumes:

```text
Response Allowance
```

---

# 9. TRUST & SAFETY DOMAIN

This answers:

> **What happens when people don't behave properly?**

### Models

- **Review**
- **Rating**
- **Dispute**
- **Evidence**
- **Report**
- **Moderation Action**
- **User Reputation**

For example:

```text
ORDER
  │
  ├── Completed
  │      │
  │      └── Review
  │
  └── Problem
         │
         ▼
      Dispute
         │
         ├── Evidence
         ├── Investigation
         └── Resolution
```

---

# 10. SELLER BUSINESS DOMAIN

There's one more domain that we shouldn't forget.

This answers:

> **How does Parts & Parcel make money from sellers?**

### Models

- **Subscription Plan**
- **Seller Subscription**
- **Subscription Entitlement**
- **Response Allowance**
- **Usage**
- Possibly **Storefront**

For example:

```text
PLAN
 │
 ├── Response allowance
 ├── Listing allowance
 ├── Storefront
 ├── Analytics
 └── Other features
        │
        ▼
SELLER SUBSCRIPTION
        │
        ▼
USAGE
```

---

# Now let's connect everything

Here's the bigger mind-map.

```text
                                ┌──────────────┐
                                │     USER     │
                                └──────┬───────┘
                                       │
                     ┌─────────────────┼─────────────────┐
                     │                 │                 │

                 BUYER             SELLER            COMMUNITY

                     │                 │            DISCUSSION

                     │                 │                 │

                     │           SUBSCRIPTION            │
                     │                 │                 │
                     │                 └───────┐         │
                     │                         │         │
                     └─────────────┐           │         │
                                   │           │         │
                                   ▼           ▼         ▼
                              MARKETPLACE / TRANSACTION
                                         │
                    ┌────────────────────┼────────────────────┐
                    │                    │                    │
                    ▼                    ▼                    ▼
                 LISTING               CART                 OFFER
                    │                    │                    │
                    │                    │                    ▼

                    │                    │                    │

                                 │

                                 │
                 ┌───────────────┼────────────────┐

                 │               │                │
                 │        ┌──────┴──────┐         │
                 │        │             │         ▼
                 │        ▼             ▼     PROTECTED
                 │     SHIPMENT       PICKUP     FUNDS
                 │        │             │         │
                 │        └──────┬──────┘         │
                 │               │                │
                 │               ▼                │
                 │            DELIVERY            │
                 │                                │
                 └────────────────┬───────────────┘
                                  │
                                  ▼
                              COMPLETION
                                  │
                       ┌──────────┼──────────┐
                       │          │          │
                       ▼          ▼          ▼
                    PAYOUT     REVIEW     DISPUTE
                       │                     │
                       ▼                     ▼
                 PLATFORM FEE          RETURN / REFUND
```

---

# But there is an even more important relationship

Everything starts with the **Catalog** and ends with the **Asset**.

Think of it as:

```text
                         CATALOG
                            │
                     "What is it?"
                            │
                            ▼
                          MODEL
                            │
                            ▼
                         ASSET
                    "Which physical one?"
                            │
                            ▼
                       COMPONENT
                            │
                            ▼
                        INVENTORY
                    "Is it available?"
                            │
                            ▼
                        LISTING
                   "Is it for sale?"
                            │
                            ▼
                     TRANSACTION
                            │
                            ▼
                          ORDER
                            │
                            ▼
                       FULFILLMENT
                            │
                            ▼
                       COMPLETION
```

And this is precisely what makes **Parts & Parcel different from ordinary ecommerce**.

Amazon-style ecommerce mostly thinks:

> Product → Inventory → Order.

We're dealing with:

> **Catalog Model → Physical Asset → Components → Inventory → Listing → Offer/Cart → Order → Fulfillment → Asset transformation.**

That last part is the killer.

---

# The Asset Transformation

Take Abel's B4.

Initially:

```text
ASSET B4
│
├── Screen
├── Motherboard
├── RAM
├── SSD
├── Battery
└── Keyboard
```

Motherboard sells.

Then:

```text
ASSET B4
│
├── Screen
├── ~~Motherboard~~ SOLD
├── RAM
├── SSD
├── Battery
└── Keyboard
```

Then Abel might decide:

> Sell RAM, SSD, battery and keyboard together.

Or:

> Sell each separately.

Or:

> Reassemble the remaining parts into a device.

That means our marketplace isn't merely tracking **products**.

It's tracking **the lifecycle of physical things**.

That is the architectural heart of Parts & Parcel.

---

# And here's where I think we should pause

We now have enough concepts.

**I don't want to add any more models yet.**

The next thing I'd suggest is that we take this map and perform a **Domain Model Cleanup**.

For every model currently on the map, we'll classify it as:

### A. Definitely a domain entity
Something that genuinely needs its own identity/lifecycle.

### B. Probably a value object
Something that doesn't need its own independent lifecycle.

### C. Relationship/pivot
Something that primarily connects two entities.

### D. State, not a model
Something we previously called a model but is really just a status.

### E. Not actually needed
A concept that can be represented by another entity.

For example, **Response Allowance** might turn out not to be an independent business entity at all; it could be an entitlement + usage record.

Likewise, **Pickup** might not need to be a separate model from Fulfillment.

And **Listing Item** might or might not be necessary depending on whether one listing can expose multiple inventory units/components.

---

## So our sequence should now be:

**1. Domain map** ✅ — where we are now.

**2. Domain model cleanup** ← **next**

**3. Connect the surviving entities and define cardinalities**
> User → many Assets  
> Asset → many Components  
> Model → many Assets  
> Listing → Inventory  
> Offer → Listing/Asset  
> Order → Order Items  
> etc.

**4. Run Abel B1–B4 through the model**

**5. Run the financial/transaction state machine**

**6. Design the UX around the validated model**

**7. Only then design the database/migrations.**

I strongly recommend we follow that order. **We're at the point where conceptual clarity is more valuable than writing code.**

### Message 30: **ASSISTANT (ChatGPT)**

Yes — you're very close, but there is one important correction that will make the model much clearer:

> **The laptop itself is the Asset. Its battery, motherboard, screen, RAM, etc. are Components of that Asset.**

However, **when a component is separated and becomes independently sellable**, it can become an **independent inventory/asset unit in the marketplace**. We need to distinguish the *physical identity* from the *way it is currently being sold*.

Let's visualize it with Abel's laptop.

---

# 1. The simplest case: complete laptop

Suppose Abel owns one HP EliteBook.

```text
CATEGORY
   │
   ▼
LAPTOP
   │
   ▼
MODEL
   │
   ▼
HP EliteBook 840 G5
   │
   ▼
ASSET
   │
   ├── Asset ID: A1001
   ├── Serial No: XYZ
   ├── RAM: 16GB
   ├── Storage: 512GB SSD
   └── Condition: Used
```

So:

```text
Category
   │
   └── Model
        │
        └── Asset
```

There is **one physical laptop**.

---

# 2. But the Asset contains components

The physical laptop is actually composed of several physical things:

```text
                    ASSET A1001
                HP EliteBook 840 G5
                       Serial XYZ
                          │
        ┌─────────────────┼─────────────────┐
        │                 │                 │
        ▼                 ▼                 ▼
   COMPONENT          COMPONENT          COMPONENT
   Motherboard          Screen             Battery
        │                 │                 │
        ▼                 ▼                 ▼
     RAM/SSD          Keyboard          Other parts
```

So conceptually:

```text
MODEL
  │
  └── ASSET
       │
       ├── Component
       ├── Component
       ├── Component
       ├── Component
       └── Component
```

This is the model I think we should preserve.

---

# 3. Now Abel wants to sell the whole laptop

He creates a listing:

> **HP EliteBook 840 G5 — 16GB RAM — 512GB SSD**  
> ₦500,000

The relationship is:

```text
CATEGORY
   │
   ▼
MODEL
   │
   ▼
ASSET
   │
   ▼
LISTING
```

The listing points to the **whole physical asset**.

The buyer purchases:

> **Asset A1001**

And all its components go with it.

---

# 4. Now the interesting case: Abel wants to sell components

Suppose the laptop is damaged.

```text
ASSET A1001
HP EliteBook
Serial XYZ
        │
        ├── Screen ❌
        ├── Motherboard ✓
        ├── Battery ✓
        ├── RAM ✓
        └── SSD ✓
```

Abel decides:

> "I'm not selling the laptop as a complete device. I'm selling its usable components."

Now we can have:

```text
                    ASSET A1001
                         │
          ┌──────────────┼──────────────┐
          │              │              │
          ▼              ▼              ▼
      COMPONENT       COMPONENT      COMPONENT
      Motherboard       Battery          RAM
          │              │              │
          ▼              ▼              ▼
       LISTING         LISTING         LISTING
       ₦70,000         ₦25,000         ₦40,000
```

Notice something important:

### The components are still components of Asset A1001.

We haven't magically created three new laptops.

They are physical components that came from that laptop.

---

# 5. Now let's introduce the word "asset" carefully

This is where your question becomes important.

You suggested:

> Model → Assets → Asset1(Battery), Asset2(Motherboard), Asset3(Screen)

I **wouldn't model it that way initially**.

I'd model:

```text
MODEL
  │
  └── ASSET A1001
        │
        ├── COMPONENT C1 — Battery
        ├── COMPONENT C2 — Motherboard
        ├── COMPONENT C3 — Screen
        ├── COMPONENT C4 — RAM
        └── COMPONENT C5 — SSD
```

Because these components have a **physical origin**.

The motherboard isn't simply an independent generic asset.

It is:

> **This particular motherboard that was originally inside Asset A1001.**

That history matters.

---

# 6. But what happens when the motherboard is removed?

This is where our model needs to become more sophisticated.

Suppose Abel sells the motherboard.

Physically:

```text
BEFORE

Laptop A1001
   │
   ├── Motherboard C2
   ├── Battery C1
   ├── Screen C3
   └── RAM C4
```

After removing the motherboard:

```text
AFTER

Laptop A1001
   │
   ├── Battery C1
   ├── Screen C3
   └── RAM C4

Motherboard C2
   │
   └── now physically separate
```

At this point, C2 is no longer physically inside A1001.

So the **component relationship changes**.

It might become:

```text
                    MODEL
                      │
             HP EliteBook 840 G5
                      │
          ┌───────────┴───────────┐
          │                       │
          ▼                       ▼
      ASSET A1001              ASSET C2?
       Remaining                Motherboard
       laptop
          │
          ├── Battery
          ├── Screen
          └── RAM
```

And **this is where I think your "asset" intuition is actually pointing toward an important concept**.

---

# 7. We need to distinguish "component" from "independent physical item"

I would therefore refine our terminology.

### Component

A physical part **within another asset**.

Example:

> Motherboard inside Abel's EliteBook.

### Independent Asset

A physical item that exists independently and can be owned, inventoried, sold, transferred, etc.

Example:

> The motherboard after Abel removes it.

So the lifecycle can be:

```text
                  ASSET A1001
                Complete Laptop
                      │
                      │ contains
                      ▼
                COMPONENT C2
                  Motherboard
                      │
                      │ separated
                      ▼
              INDEPENDENT ASSET
                  Motherboard
                      │
                      ▼
                   LISTING
                      │
                      ▼
                    SALE
```

**This is a much more powerful model.**

---

# 8. But we shouldn't create a new physical asset unnecessarily

Suppose Abel has a laptop and simply lists it for parts without actually dismantling it.

For example:

> "HP EliteBook 840 G5. Screen damaged. Selling for parts."

We don't need to physically create five independent assets.

We can still have:

```text
ASSET A1001
   │
   ├── Screen
   ├── Motherboard
   ├── Battery
   ├── RAM
   └── SSD
```

and the listing can say:

> **Parts available from this asset**

Then when someone buys the motherboard:

> The motherboard becomes separated from A1001.

---

# 9. This gives us a very useful lifecycle

```text
             COMPLETE ASSET
                  │
                  ▼
        ┌─────────────────────┐
        │ Components attached │
        └──────────┬──────────┘
                   │
              Seller lists
              whole device
                   │
              ┌────┴────┐
              │         │
             SOLD     For Parts
                        │
                        ▼
                Component Listing
                        │
                     SOLD?
                        │
              ┌─────────┴─────────┐
              │                   │
             NO                  YES
              │                   │
              │              Separate component
              │                   │
              │                   ▼
              │             Independent Asset
              │                   │
              │                   ▼
              │                Listing
              │                   │
              │                   ▼
              │                  SALE
              │
              ▼
       Asset remains intact
```

---

# 10. Now let's apply it to Abel's four laptops

### Laptop 1

Screen bad.

Abel sells the **whole laptop**.

```text
Model
 │
 └── Asset A1
       │
       ├── Screen ❌
       ├── Motherboard
       ├── RAM
       └── ...
             │
             ▼
          Listing
             │
             ▼
           SOLD
```

Simple.

---

### Laptop 2

Motherboard bad.

Abel wants to sell:

- screen
- hard disk
- RAM

individually.

```text
Asset A2
 │
 ├── Motherboard ❌
 ├── Screen ─────── Listing
 ├── Hard Disk ───── Listing
 ├── RAM ─────────── Listing
 └── ...
```

They remain components until actually separated/transferred.

---

### Laptop 3

Keyboard bad.

He lists parts.

Someone buys the screen.

Now:

```text
Asset A3
 │
 ├── Keyboard ❌
 ├── Screen ───── SOLD → separated
 ├── RAM
 ├── SSD
 └── Battery
```

Then Abel says:

> "I'll sell the remaining laptop as a whole."

We can create a **new sellable configuration** from the remaining components:

```text
Remaining Asset
 │
 ├── RAM
 ├── SSD
 ├── Battery
 └── ...
        │
        ▼
     Listing
```

The physical history is preserved.

---

### Laptop 4

This is the really important one.

```text
Asset A4
 │
 ├── Screen ❌
 ├── Motherboard
 ├── RAM
 ├── SSD
 └── Battery
```

Whole laptop listed.

Buyer makes offer:

> "I'll buy only the motherboard for ₦70k."

Abel accepts.

Now:

```text
Asset A4
 │
 ├── Screen ❌
 ├── Motherboard ─── SOLD
 ├── RAM
 ├── SSD
 └── Battery
```

The motherboard becomes an independent physical item.

Then Abel can:

### Option A

Sell RAM individually.

### Option B

Sell SSD individually.

### Option C

Sell remaining components as a package.

### Option D

Create a listing for the remaining laptop.

The model supports all four.

---

# 11. The key relationship I think you're looking for

I'd visualize it like this:

```text
                         CATEGORY
                            │
                            │ 1:N
                            ▼
                           MODEL
                            │
                            │ 1:N
                            ▼
                          ASSET
                            │
                    ┌───────┴────────┐
                    │                │
                    │ contains       │
                    ▼                │
                COMPONENT            │
                    │                │
                    │ separated      │
                    ▼                │
              INDEPENDENT ASSET ◄────┘
                    │
                    │ can be listed
                    ▼
                  LISTING
                    │
                    ▼
               TRANSACTION
                    │
                    ▼
                  ORDER
```

But there's an important detail:

### `MODEL → ASSET`

means:

> "This physical thing is an instance of this catalog model."

### `ASSET → COMPONENT`

means:

> "This physical thing currently contains this physical component."

### `COMPONENT → INDEPENDENT ASSET`

means:

> "This component has been separated and now exists independently."

That is the conceptual relationship we need to explore.

---

# 12. There is one question we should settle before going further

Your example says:

> `category → model → assets → asset1(Battery), asset2(Motherboard), asset3(Screen)`

The question is actually:

### **Do we want every physical component to eventually become an Asset with its own identity?**

For example:

```text
Laptop A1001
   │
   ├── Battery B1001
   ├── Motherboard M1001
   └── Screen S1001
```

If the motherboard is sold:

```text
M1001
Owner: Abel
Origin: A1001
Status: Available
```

Then sold:

```text
M1001
Owner: Buyer X
Origin: A1001
Status: Sold
```

This gives us **complete provenance**.

And for Parts & Parcel, I think that could be extremely valuable.

Because eventually someone might want to know:

> "Where did this motherboard come from?"

Answer:

> "Originally removed from an HP EliteBook 840 G5, serial XYZ."

That's especially useful for used/refurbished parts.

**But I don't want to declare this final yet.** This is exactly the kind of question we should resolve during the domain-model cleanup before we design the database.

### Message 32: **ASSISTANT (ChatGPT)**

Yes. **Your idea solves the conceptual problem of what the buyer interacts with**, and I think it gives us a much cleaner direction.

But I would make one small refinement before we freeze it:

> **A Listing should represent the seller's offer to the marketplace, and it can point to either an Asset or a Component.**
>
> The buyer never needs to know whether they are buying an "asset" or a "component" as a technical concept. They see a **Listing**.

So conceptually:

```text
                         CATALOG
                            │
                 ┌──────────┴──────────┐
                 │                     │
              CATEGORY               MODEL
                 │                     │
                 └──────────┬──────────┘
                            │
                            ▼
                          ASSET
                     HP EliteBook XYZ
                            │
                 ┌──────────┼──────────┐
                 │          │          │
                 ▼          ▼          ▼
              Battery    Motherboard  Screen
             COMPONENT    COMPONENT   COMPONENT
                 │          │          │
                 └──────┬───┴──────────┘
                        │
                        ▼
                     LISTING
                        │
              ┌─────────┴─────────┐
              │                   │
       Asset Listing       Component Listing
              │                   │
              ▼                   ▼
       "HP EliteBook"       "EliteBook
        ₦500,000             Motherboard
                              ₦70,000"
```

And **this is what the buyer browses**.

---

# But there's one important wrinkle: Seth

Seth has:

> 10 HP EliteBook batteries.

Those aren't necessarily components currently sitting inside ten laptops.

They're standalone stock.

So we need the model to support:

```text
Listing
   │
   ├── Asset
   │
   └── Component
```

**and also inventory that exists as a quantity of identical standalone items.**

This is why I don't want us to simply put `asset_id` and `component_id` on Listing and call the whole architecture finished.

The Listing relationship is correct, but **Inventory still needs to sit underneath it.**

We'll resolve that properly during the domain-model cleanup.

---

# Now, the complete roadmap

I agree with you completely that we should stop adding concepts and get the whole project into a controlled sequence.

Here is the order I recommend from **where we are now all the way to migrations and UI**.

---

# PHASE 1 — Domain Model Cleanup

### 1. Inventory/Asset/Component architecture
We need to settle precisely:

```text
Catalog
   ↓
Model
   ↓
Asset
   ↓
Component
```

and determine when a component remains a component versus when it becomes independently owned/managed.

We'll also solve Seth's:

> 10 identical batteries

case.

### 2. Listing architecture

Finalize:

```text
Listing
   ├── Asset
   └── Component
```

and determine how quantity, price, condition and availability are represented.

### 3. Identify the final entities

Go through every concept we've mentioned and classify it:

- Entity
- Value object
- Relationship
- Attribute
- State
- Event
- Probably unnecessary

This should eliminate unnecessary models before we code.

---

# PHASE 2 — Relationship Map

Then we'll create the actual ER/domain relationship map.

Something like:

```text
USER
 │
 ├─────────────── owns ───────────────► ASSET
 │                                       │
 │                                       └── has ──► COMPONENT
 │
 ├─────────────── creates ─────────────► LISTING
 │
 ├─────────────── creates ─────────────► DISCUSSION
 │                                       │
 │                                       └── has ──► RESPONSE
 │
 └─────────────── has ─────────────────► SUBSCRIPTION
```

And:

```text
MODEL ──────► ASSET
                 │
                 ▼
             COMPONENT
                 │
                 ▼
              LISTING
                 │
                 ├──── OFFER
                 ├──── CART
                 │
                 ▼
               ORDER
                 │
          ┌──────┼───────┐
          ▼      ▼       ▼
       PAYMENT FULFILLMENT DISPUTE
          │      │
          ▼      ▼
       PAYOUT  DELIVERY
```

We'll define the **cardinality of every relationship**.

For example:

> One User can own many Assets.

> One Asset can have many Components.

> One Listing represents one sellable target.

> One Order has many Order Items.

etc.

---

# PHASE 3 — Lifecycle & State Machines

This is where our earlier work comes back.

We'll define the lifecycle of:

### Asset

```text
Active
   ↓
Listed
   ↓
Partially dismantled
   ↓
Separated
   ↓
Transferred / Sold
```

### Listing

```text
Draft
 ↓
Published
 ↓
Unavailable
 ↓
Sold / Closed
```

### Offer

```text
Sent
 ↓
Accepted / Rejected / Expired
```

with your important rule:

```text
Seller sends offer to A + B
             │
       A accepts first
             │
             ▼
         Reserved
             │
       B accepts later
             │
             ▼
          Pending
             │
     A payment expires
             │
             ▼
      B automatically
       gets reservation
```

### Cart/Checkout

We'll explicitly decide that:

> **Adding to cart does not reserve inventory.**

### Inventory allocation

We'll define the atomic mechanism that prevents:

> two successful orders buying the same physical asset.

### Order

```text
Pending Payment
Paid
Processing
Fulfilled
Delivered
Completed
Cancelled
Disputed
Returned
Refunded
```

But we won't blindly use these states; we'll derive the actual states from the workflows.

---

# PHASE 4 — Abel's Four-Laptop Test

This is our **domain-model test suite**.

We'll run:

### Abel 1
Whole laptop sale.

### Abel 2
Individual components.

### Abel 3
Screen sold → remaining components become sellable as a whole.

### Abel 4
Whole listing → motherboard offer → motherboard sold → remaining components listed.

If the model can't represent these cleanly, we redesign it.

We should also test:

### Adam

```text
3 EliteBooks
2 HP Envy
3 Dell
2 Acer
```

to test multiple identical assets.

### Seth

```text
10 HP batteries
7 Dell batteries
...
```

to test quantity inventory.

Those three sellers together are effectively our **domain-model stress test**.

---

# PHASE 5 — Buyer Experience

Only after the model works do we design the buyer UX.

We'll define:

## Browse

What exactly appears on:

- Products page
- Category page
- Model page
- Search results
- Filters
- Sorting
- Pagination

## Listing card

For example:

```text
┌──────────────────────────────┐
│     [Product Image]          │
│                              │
│ HP EliteBook 840 G5          │
│ 16GB / 512GB                 │
│ Used • Working               │
│                              │
│ ₦500,000                     │
│                              │
│ Seller: Abel                 │
│ [Buy Now]                    │
└──────────────────────────────┘
```

For a component:

```text
┌──────────────────────────────┐
│     [Motherboard Image]      │
│                              │
│ HP EliteBook 840 G5           │
│ Motherboard                   │
│ Tested • Working              │
│                              │
│ ₦70,000                       │
│                              │
│ Seller: Abel                 │
│ [Buy Now] [Make Offer]        │
└──────────────────────────────┘
```

The buyer doesn't need to understand the database distinction.

---

# PHASE 6 — Listing Detail UX

We'll design the complete listing page.

For a complete device:

```text
Photos
Title
Price
Condition
Specifications
Model
Seller
Availability
Description
Defects
Components/specifications
Delivery
Buy Now
Add to Cart
Make Offer
```

For a component:

```text
Photos
Part name
Compatible models
Condition
Test status
Price
Quantity
Origin/provenance where useful
Seller
Delivery
Buy Now
Add to Cart
Make Offer
```

And importantly:

> **The listing page should be able to explain compatibility.**

That's one of the major advantages of our Catalog.

---

# PHASE 7 — Seller UX

Then we'll design how Adam, Abel and Seth actually use the platform.

### Seller dashboard

```text
My Business
│
├── Listings
├── Inventory
├── Assets
├── Orders
├── Offers
├── Discussions
├── Responses
├── Returns
├── Payments
├── Payouts
└── Subscription
```

Then we'll simplify this for the actual user interface.

The technical nomenclature does **not** necessarily have to be exposed.

For example, instead of:

> "Create Asset"

the seller might see:

> **Add Item**

Instead of:

> "Create Component"

they might see:

> **Add Part**

We'll keep the technical model sophisticated while keeping the UX simple.

---

# PHASE 8 — Seller Listing Workflow

This deserves its own design.

For example:

### "What are you selling?"

```text
What are you selling?

○ Complete device
○ Part / component
○ Multiple identical items
```

Then the platform guides them.

For a complete laptop:

```text
Category → Laptop
Brand → HP
Model → EliteBook 840 G5
Condition → Used
Serial number → XYZ
Specifications → ...
Photos → ...
Price → ₦500,000
```

For Abel's damaged laptop:

```text
What would you like to sell?

○ The whole laptop
○ Parts from this laptop
```

Then Parts & Parcel can guide him rather than forcing him to understand "Asset vs Component."

---

# PHASE 9 — Forum & Community

Then we'll separately finalize:

```text
Discussion
   │
   └── Response
          │
          └── Offer
```

And:

```text
Subscription
      │
      ▼
Entitlements
      │
      ▼
Response allowance
      │
      ▼
Usage
```

We'll design:

- discussion creation
- response limits
- offer responses
- attachments
- moderation
- spam detection
- subscription prompts
- expired allowance
- abuse prevention
- anti-circumvention mechanisms

---

# PHASE 10 — Financial Architecture

Then we return to the question you raised earlier:

> **Who pays whom?**

We'll finalize:

```text
Buyer
  │
  ▼
Parts & Parcel
  │
  ▼
Protected payment
  │
  ├── Platform fee
  │
  └── Seller payout
```

and decide exactly which transactions use:

- platform checkout
- escrow/protected payment
- seller subscription
- commission
- refund
- payout
- withdrawal

We'll also model:

```text
Payment
Payment attempt
Fee
Refund
Payout
```

separately where necessary.

---

# PHASE 11 — Fulfillment

Then:

```text
ORDER
  │
  ▼
FULFILLMENT
  │
  ├── Shipment
  │
  └── Pickup
```

We'll define:

- seller preparation
- courier integration
- tracking
- pickup
- delivery confirmation
- failed delivery
- return
- replacement
- dispute

This is especially important because you previously established that **shipment isn't always present**.

---

# PHASE 12 — Trust, Disputes & Returns

Then we'll design:

```text
ORDER
 │
 ├── normal completion
 │
 └── problem
       │
       ▼
    DISPUTE
       │
       ├── Evidence
       ├── Investigation
       └── Resolution
             │
       ┌─────┴─────┐
       ▼           ▼
    Refund       Seller wins
       │
       ▼
    Return
```

We'll make sure partial disputes work.

---

# PHASE 13 — Database Design

**Only now do we write migrations.**

At this point we'll produce:

### Core tables

Users and identity.

### Catalog tables

```text
categories
brands
models
component_types
model_components
```

### Physical inventory

```text
assets
components
inventory...
```

### Marketplace

```text
listings
offers
carts
cart_items
```

### Transactions

```text
orders
order_items
...
```

### Finance

```text
payments
payment_transactions
fees
payouts
refunds
```

### Fulfillment

```text
fulfillments
shipments
returns
...
```

### Community

```text
discussions
responses
...
```

### Subscription

```text
plans
subscriptions
entitlements
usage
...
```

### Trust

```text
disputes
evidence
reviews
reports
...
```

But **we will not assume these table names or even that every one needs to exist** until the preceding phases are finished.

---

# PHASE 14 — Database Integrity Pass

Then we'll do the thing we discussed in your other schema work:

### Integrity pass

We will ask:

- Can the same physical asset be sold twice?
- Can the same component be sold twice?
- Can a component belong to two assets simultaneously?
- Can inventory become negative?
- Can two buyers win the same offer?
- Can two accepted offers reserve the same asset?
- What happens when reservation expires?
- What happens when payment succeeds but order creation fails?
- What happens when payout fails?
- Can an order contain an unavailable item?
- Can a seller modify an item after payment?
- What happens when a component is removed from an asset?
- Can a listing point to something no longer owned by the seller?
- Can a component be listed while its parent asset is also sold?
- What happens to the remaining components after a partial sale?

This is where we'll discover the final constraints and indexes.

---

# PHASE 15 — Laravel Implementation

Then we translate the domain into Laravel:

- migrations
- Eloquent models
- relationships
- enums
- casts
- policies
- services/actions
- repositories only where actually useful
- events
- listeners
- jobs
- notifications
- database transactions
- locking
- validation
- authorization

Especially the **inventory allocation operation**, which must be concurrency-safe.

---

# PHASE 16 — Views / UX Implementation

Then we build the actual Blade/Livewire interfaces.

### Buyer

```text
Home
→ Categories
→ Models
→ Listings
→ Listing Detail
→ Cart
→ Checkout
→ Payment
→ Orders
→ Discussions
```

### Seller

```text
Dashboard
→ Inventory
→ Add Item
→ Create Listing
→ Offers
→ Orders
→ Fulfillment
→ Payouts
→ Subscription
```

### Community

```text
Discussions
→ Discussion
→ Responses
→ Offer
→ Transaction
```

---

# PHASE 17 — Admin

Then:

```text
Admin
│
├── Users
├── Sellers
├── Catalog
├── Listings
├── Assets
├── Orders
├── Payments
├── Payouts
├── Disputes
├── Returns
├── Discussions
├── Moderation
├── Subscriptions
└── Reports
```

---

# PHASE 18 — Testing

Finally, we create scenario-based tests.

Not just:

> "Does ListingController work?"

but:

### Scenario 1

Adam has 3 EliteBooks → three buyers buy one each → fourth buyer fails.

### Scenario 2

Abel's motherboard → A and B accept offer → A gets reservation → B pending → A expires → B gets reservation.

### Scenario 3

Abel sells motherboard → remaining components become independently listable.

### Scenario 4

Seth has 10 batteries → concurrent purchases don't produce negative inventory.

### Scenario 5

Buyer pays → seller fails to fulfill → dispute → refund.

### Scenario 6

Buyer receives three components → disputes one → partial resolution.

Those tests will be much more valuable than simply testing individual CRUD screens.

---

## And our immediate next task is **#1: Domain Cleanup**

I would **not write a migration yet**.

Our immediate goal should be to take every concept we've accumulated and produce one authoritative table:

| Domain | Concept | Keep? | What it represents | Relationship |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Catalog | Category | ? | ... | ... |
| Catalog | Model | ? | ... | ... |
| Inventory | Asset | ? | ... | ... |
| Inventory | Component | ? | ... | ... |
| Marketplace | Listing | ? | ... | ... |
| Transactions | Offer | ? | ... | ... |
| Transactions | Reservation | ? | ... | ... |
| ... | ... | ... | ... | ... |

**That is the next thing I would do.**

And specifically, I would start by resolving the **Asset → Component → Inventory → Listing** relationship, including Seth's 10 batteries, because that is the foundation on which almost everything else depends. Once that is clean, the rest of the architecture will become dramatically easier to conceptualize.

### Message 38: **ASSISTANT (ChatGPT)**

Absolutely. And I think **now is the right time** to do this, because the UI has given us something concrete to map the database against.

I would **not call this the final migration yet**. Let's first build the database blueprint and trace real user activities through it. Then we can challenge the model before writing migrations.

The key principle I would use is:

> **The database should model what actually exists and what actually happens—not mirror the UI terminology.**

So, for example, the buyer sees a "listing", but the listing is not the thing itself. It is the **commercial presentation of something the seller owns or controls**.

---

# 1. The big picture

I would currently divide Parts & Parcel into these domains:

```text
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                        PARTS & PARCEL                       │
│                                                             │
│  IDENTITY        CATALOG          INVENTORY / OWNERSHIP     │
│     │               │                    │                  │
│     ▼               ▼                    ▼                  │
│   Users        Categories              Assets               │
│   Profiles     Brands                  Components            │
│   Sellers      Models                  Inventory             │
│                                                             │
│                         │                                   │
│                         ▼                                   │
│                    MARKETPLACE                              │
│                         │                                   │
│                    Listings                                 │
│                         │                                   │
│             ┌───────────┴───────────┐                       │
│             ▼                       ▼                       │
│          Offers                  Wishlist                    │
│             │                                               │
│             ▼                                               │
│           Orders                                             │
│             │                                               │
│       ┌─────┴──────┐                                        │
│       ▼            ▼                                        │
│    Payments      Fulfilment                                 │
│                                                             │
│                         COMMUNITY                           │
│                             │                               │
│                       Discussions                           │
│                             │                               │
│                         Responses                           │
│                             │                               │
│                           Offers                             │
│                                                             │
│                    PLATFORM / BILLING                       │
│                             │                               │
│                    Subscriptions                            │
│                    Transactions                             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

But there is an important distinction:

**Not all of these are equally fundamental.**

The heart of the system is:

```text
CATALOG
   ↓
ASSET / COMPONENT
   ↓
INVENTORY
   ↓
LISTING
   ↓
OFFER
   ↓
ORDER
   ↓
PAYMENT / FULFILMENT
```

The forum, wishlist, subscriptions, etc. sit around this core.

---

# 2. Domain 1 — Identity

This handles people and their roles.

## `users`

The actual account.

| Field | Purpose |
|---|---|
| id | PK |
| name | User's name |
| email | Login/contact |
| phone | Phone |
| password | Authentication |
| status | active/suspended/etc. |
| created_at | |
| updated_at | |

A user can be:

```text
Buyer
Seller
Forum participant
Both Buyer + Seller
```

We **don't need separate buyer and seller users**.

That's important.

Adam can sell today and buy tomorrow.

---

## `seller_profiles`

Seller-specific information.

| Field | Purpose |
|---|---|
| id | PK |
| user_id | FK → users |
| display_name | Seller name |
| description | Seller bio |
| location_id | Seller location |
| status | active/suspended |
| rating | Derived/cache |
| created_at | |
| updated_at | |

Relationship:

```text
users
  │
  └── 0..1 seller_profile
```

---

# 3. Domain 2 — Catalog

This describes **what things are**, independent of who owns them.

This is where your original:

> Category → Brand → Model → Device

idea becomes useful.

---

## `categories`

Examples:

```text
Laptop
Phone
Washing Machine
Generator
Construction Equipment
```

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| parent_id |
| name |
| slug |
| description |
| status |

`parent_id` allows:

```text
Electronics
 ├── Computers
 │    ├── Laptops
 │    └── Desktops
 └── Phones
```

---

## `brands`

Examples:

```text
HP
Dell
Acer
Apple
Samsung
```

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| name |
| slug |
| status |

---

## `models`

Examples:

```text
HP EliteBook 840 G5
Dell Latitude 1500
iPhone 12
```

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| category_id |
| brand_id |
| name |
| slug |
| description |
| status |

Relationship:

```text
Category
   │
   └── Model
        │
        └── Brand
```

More accurately:

```text
Category ──────< Models >────── Brand
```

---

# 4. Domain 3 — Parts / Component Catalog

This is where we need to be careful.

A **component type** is not the same thing as a physical component.

For example:

> "HP EliteBook 840 G5 Battery"

is a catalog definition.

Whereas:

> "The actual battery removed from Abel's laptop"

is a physical component.

So we need a catalog-level concept.

## `component_types`

| Field | Purpose |
|---|---|
| id | PK |
| name | Battery, Screen, RAM, Motherboard |
| category_id | General component category |
| description | |
| status | |

---

## `model_components`

This establishes which components belong to a model.

Example:

```text
HP EliteBook 840 G5
       │
       ├── Motherboard
       ├── Screen
       ├── Battery
       ├── RAM
       ├── Keyboard
       └── Hard Drive
```

Table:

### `model_components`

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| model_id |
| component_type_id |
| name |
| description |
| status |

This lets us answer:

> What parts are compatible with an HP EliteBook 840 G5?

without looking at somebody's inventory.

---

# 5. Domain 4 — Physical Inventory

This is arguably the **most important domain in Parts & Parcel**.

Because we aren't simply selling abstract products.

We're dealing with **physical things**.

---

# `assets`

An asset represents a physical device.

Example:

```text
Abel's laptop
HP EliteBook 840 G5
Serial XYZ
```

| Field | Purpose |
|---|---|
| id | PK |
| owner_id | FK → users |
| model_id | FK → models |
| serial_number | nullable |
| condition | new/used/etc. |
| status | active/sold/etc. |
| acquired_at | |
| created_at | |
| updated_at | |

So Adam's inventory could look like:

```text
assets

A1 → HP EliteBook 840 G5
A2 → HP EliteBook 840 G5
A3 → HP EliteBook 840 G5

A4 → HP Envy
A5 → HP Envy

A6 → Dell Latitude 1500
A7 → Dell Latitude 1500
...
```

Even though A1, A2 and A3 have the same model, they are **three physical laptops**.

---

# 6. `components`

This represents an actual physical component.

For example, Abel's damaged laptop:

```text
Asset A10
HP EliteBook 840 G5
Serial XYZ
       │
       ├── Component C1 → Screen
       ├── Component C2 → Motherboard
       ├── Component C3 → RAM
       ├── Component C4 → Battery
       └── Component C5 → Keyboard
```

Fields:

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| asset_id |
| model_component_id |
| serial_number |
| condition |
| status |
| created_at |
| updated_at |

And this gives us the critical relationship:

```text
Asset
  │
  └── Components
```

---

# 7. But what happens when a component is separated?

This is one of the reasons we needed to think carefully about this system.

Suppose:

```text
Abel's Laptop A10
       │
       ├── Screen C1
       ├── Motherboard C2
       ├── RAM C3
       └── Battery C4
```

Abel sells the motherboard.

We don't delete C2.

Instead:

```text
C2
Motherboard
status = sold
```

And potentially its ownership changes after the transaction.

The original asset can now become:

```text
A10
HP EliteBook
status = dismantled / partially_disassembled
```

while the remaining components remain associated with it.

This is exactly why **asset and component must be separate concepts**.

---

# 8. Domain 5 — Inventory

Now we get to an important distinction.

**Asset/component = what physically exists.**

**Inventory = how it is currently available for sale.**

For example, Seth doesn't necessarily need ten separate "device assets" for ten identical batteries.

He can have:

```text
HP EliteBook Battery
Quantity = 10
```

So I would currently model:

## `inventory_items`

| Field | Purpose |
|---|---|
| id | PK |
| seller_id | FK |
| model_id | nullable |
| component_type_id | nullable |
| inventory_type | asset/component/stock |
| quantity | available quantity |
| reserved_quantity | reserved |
| sold_quantity | sold |
| status | active/inactive |
| created_at |
| updated_at |

But **I would not yet freeze this table**.

There is an important question here:

> Do we really need `inventory_items` to hold the quantity itself, or should inventory be derived from the underlying assets/components and stock records?

We'll test this when we write the actual flows.

---

# 9. Domain 6 — Listings

This is the object the **buyer actually sees**.

This is the concept you proposed earlier, and I agree with it.

```text
Asset / Component / Stock
          │
          ▼
       LISTING
          │
          ▼
        Buyer
```

## `listings`

| Field | Purpose |
|---|---|
| id | PK |
| seller_id | FK |
| title | Display title |
| description | Description |
| listing_type | device / part / etc. |
| price | Asking price |
| condition | Condition |
| quantity | Available |
| status | draft/published/sold/etc. |
| published_at | |
| created_at |
| updated_at |

But we need the relationship to the thing being sold.

You suggested:

```text
listing
   ├── asset_id
   └── component_id
```

I think that is a reasonable starting point.

But I would probably make it more explicit eventually:

```text
listing
   │
   ├── asset
   │
   ├── component
   │
   └── inventory_item
```

with exactly **one source** being the actual sellable thing.

That gives us:

```text
Listing
   │
   ├── Complete device → Asset
   │
   ├── Individual component → Component
   │
   └── Repeated stock → Inventory Item
```

This maps beautifully to our three sellers.

---

# 10. Adam's data

Adam has:

```text
5 HP
3 Dell
2 Acer
```

But they're actually:

```text
10 Assets
```

For example:

```text
A1 → HP EliteBook 840 G5
A2 → HP EliteBook 840 G5
A3 → HP EliteBook 840 G5

A4 → HP Envy
A5 → HP Envy

A6 → Dell Latitude 1500
A7 → Dell Latitude 1500

A8 → Dell Inspiron
A9 → Dell Inspiron

A10 → Acer ...
```

Listings could be:

```text
L1 → A1 → ₦500k
L2 → A2 → ₦500k
L3 → A3 → ₦500k
```

Or, depending on UX, we could group identical assets into one listing with quantity 3.

**This is something we should decide during the actual data-flow exercise.**

---

# 11. Abel's data

Abel has:

```text
A20 → Laptop 1
A21 → Laptop 2
A22 → Laptop 3
A23 → Laptop 4
```

Laptop 2:

```text
A21
 │
 ├── C1 Screen
 ├── C2 Motherboard
 ├── C3 RAM
 └── C4 Hard Disk
```

He can create:

```text
L10 → A21
```

if selling the whole laptop.

Or:

```text
L11 → C2 Motherboard
L12 → C3 RAM
L13 → C4 Hard Disk
```

if selling components.

---

# 12. Seth's data

Seth doesn't need:

```text
A1
A2
A3
...
A10
```

if his business is simply carrying interchangeable batteries.

Instead:

```text
Inventory Item
────────────────────────
HP EliteBook Battery
Quantity: 10
```

and:

```text
Listing
──────────────────────
HP EliteBook Battery
₦25,000
10 available
```

This is why the distinction between **physical asset** and **stock inventory** is important.

---

# 13. Domain 7 — Offers

## `offers`

An offer can originate from:

1. A listing
2. A forum response

So:

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| buyer_id |
| seller_id |
| listing_id |
| discussion_response_id |
| quantity |
| amount |
| message |
| status |
| expires_at |
| accepted_at |
| created_at |
| updated_at |

Possible states:

```text
pending
accepted
reserved
expired
rejected
cancelled
converted
```

And this connects directly to the reservation logic we discussed.

---

# 14. Domain 8 — Orders

Once money enters the picture, an accepted offer isn't the final commercial record.

We create an order.

## `orders`

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| buyer_id |
| seller_id |
| order_number |
| subtotal |
| delivery_fee |
| platform_fee |
| total |
| status |
| payment_status |
| fulfilment_status |
| created_at |
| updated_at |

Then:

## `order_items`

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| order_id |
| listing_id |
| quantity |
| unit_price |
| total |
| status |

This gives us:

```text
Order
 │
 ├── Order Item
 │       │
 │       └── Listing
 │
 └── Payment
```

---

# 15. Domain 9 — Payments

I would separate payment from orders.

## `payments`

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| order_id |
| payer_id |
| amount |
| provider |
| reference |
| status |
| paid_at |
| created_at |

Possible status:

```text
pending
successful
failed
refunded
partially_refunded
```

This becomes particularly important because we have already decided that **Parts & Parcel should be involved in checkout/payment**, rather than simply telling the buyer to transfer money directly to the seller.

---

# 16. Domain 10 — Fulfilment

This is where logistics enters.

## `shipments`

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| order_id |
| seller_id |
| buyer_id |
| delivery_method |
| courier |
| tracking_number |
| status |
| pickup_address |
| delivery_address |
| shipped_at |
| delivered_at |

Possible status:

```text
pending
ready_for_dispatch
in_transit
out_for_delivery
delivered
failed
returned
```

The seller doesn't necessarily become a logistics company.

The seller simply does:

```text
Order received
      ↓
Prepare item
      ↓
Hand item to courier
```

Parts & Parcel tracks the transaction and shipment.

---

# 17. Domain 11 — Forum

Now the community side.

## `discussions`

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| user_id |
| category_id |
| model_id |
| title |
| body |
| status |
| created_at |
| updated_at |

This lets us connect:

> "I need an HP EliteBook 840 G5 motherboard"

directly to the catalog.

---

## `discussion_responses`

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| discussion_id |
| user_id |
| body |
| response_type |
| created_at |
| updated_at |

`response_type` could be:

```text
text
offer
```

But I wouldn't necessarily make an offer a completely different response table.

Instead:

```text
Discussion
   │
   └── Responses
          │
          ├── text response
          │
          └── response + Offer
```

That fits our UX extremely well.

---

# 18. Domain 12 — Wishlist

Simple.

## `wishlists`

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| user_id |
| name |

## `wishlist_items`

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| wishlist_id |
| listing_id |
| created_at |

So:

```text
User
 │
 └── Wishlist
       │
       └── Listing
```

---

# 19. Domain 13 — Cart

The cart should be temporary purchasing state.

## `carts`

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| user_id |
| status |
| expires_at |

## `cart_items`

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| cart_id |
| listing_id |
| quantity |
| unit_price |
| created_at |
| updated_at |

And importantly:

**Cart does NOT reserve inventory.**

That agrees with the decision we reached earlier.

```text
Cart
  │
  └── "I intend to buy this"

Payment
  │
  └── "I have actually bought this"
```

The actual reservation occurs around the **offer/payment process**, where necessary.

---

# 20. Domain 14 — Subscriptions

Because we've decided the forum response allowance can be monetized.

## `subscription_plans`

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| name |
| price |
| billing_interval |
| forum_responses |
| features |
| status |

Example:

```text
Free
────
5 responses/month

Seller
──────
20 responses/month

Pro
────
100 responses/month
```

Then:

## `subscriptions`

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| user_id |
| subscription_plan_id |
| status |
| starts_at |
| ends_at |
| renews_at |
| provider |
| provider_reference |

And potentially:

## `usage_records`

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| user_id |
| subscription_id |
| feature |
| quantity |
| period_start |
| period_end |

This allows us to enforce:

> "You have 4 responses left."

without caring whether the response was:

```text
text
```

or

```text
offer
```

Both consume one response.

---

# 21. Domain 15 — Platform financial transactions

Because eventually we need to know where money went.

I would not rely solely on `payments`.

We will likely need something like:

## `financial_transactions`

| Field |
|---|
| id |
| user_id |
| order_id |
| type |
| amount |
| direction |
| status |
| reference |
| created_at |

Examples:

```text
Buyer payment       +₦70,000
Platform commission  -₦7,000
Seller payout        +₦63,000
Refund               -₦70,000
Subscription         +₦5,000
```

We don't necessarily need to implement this yet, but **the financial domain needs to exist conceptually**.

---

# 22. The relationship map

Now we can see the system much more clearly.

```text
                         ┌──────────────┐
                         │    USERS     │
                         └──────┬───────┘
                                │
                ┌───────────────┼────────────────┐
                │               │                │
                ▼               ▼                ▼
         Seller Profile       Cart          Subscription
                │               │                │
                │               ▼                ▼
                │         Cart Items       Usage Records
                │               │
                │               ▼
                │           LISTINGS
                │               ▲
                │               │
                ▼               │
             ASSETS             │
                │               │
                ▼               │
           COMPONENTS           │
                │               │
                └───────────────┘
                                │
                                ▼
                             OFFERS
                                │
                                ▼
                              ORDERS
                                │
                    ┌───────────┼───────────┐
                    ▼           ▼           ▼
                Order Items  Payments    Shipments
                    │
                    ▼
                 Listings
```

And separately:

```text
CATALOG
────────────────────────────

Category
   │
   ├── Brand
   │
   └── Model
         │
         └── Model Components
                    │
                    ▼
             Component Types
```

Then:

```text
COMMUNITY
────────────────────────────

User
 │
 ▼
Discussion
 │
 ▼
Response
 │
 └──────────────► Offer
```

And:

```text
USER
 │
 ├── Wishlist ──► Listing
 │
 ├── Cart ──────► Listing
 │
 ├── Offers
 │
 ├── Orders
 │
 ├── Discussions
 │
 └── Subscriptions
```

---

# 23. Now let's map actual user activity to tables

This is probably the most useful part for you.

## Seller lists a complete laptop

Abel:

> "I want to sell my HP EliteBook."

System:

```text
USER
 ↓
ASSET
 ↓
LISTING
```

Tables touched:

```text
users
assets
listings
```

If it already has components:

```text
assets
components
listings
```

---

## Seller lists a motherboard

```text
USER
 ↓
COMPONENT
 ↓
LISTING
```

Tables:

```text
users
components
listings
```

---

## Seth lists 10 batteries

```text
USER
 ↓
INVENTORY ITEM
 ↓
LISTING
```

Tables:

```text
users
inventory_items
listings
```

---

# 24. Buyer browses laptops

Mostly:

```text
categories
brands
models
listings
```

The buyer doesn't need to know:

```text
assets
components
inventory_items
```

That's an important UX principle.

---

# 25. Buyer opens listing

```text
listings
   │
   ├── seller
   ├── asset
   ├── component
   └── inventory
```

The listing becomes the **gateway into the underlying object**.

---

# 26. Buyer adds to cart

```text
carts
   │
   └── cart_items
             │
             ▼
          listings
```

No order yet.

No payment yet.

No sale yet.

---

# 27. Buyer makes offer

```text
users
 │
 ▼
offers
 │
 ▼
listing
```

If forum:

```text
discussion
   │
   ▼
response
   │
   ▼
offer
```

---

# 28. Seller accepts

The important state transition:

```text
Offer
pending
   ↓
accepted
   ↓
reserved
```

Then:

```text
Order
```

can be created when the buyer proceeds to payment.

---

# 29. Buyer pays

```text
Order
  │
  ▼
Payment
  │
  ▼
successful
```

Then inventory changes.

For a single asset:

```text
Asset
available
   ↓
sold / allocated
```

For Seth:

```text
Inventory
10
 ↓
9
```

---

# 30. Seller ships

```text
Order
  │
  ▼
Shipment
  │
  ▼
in_transit
  ↓
out_for_delivery
  ↓
delivered
```

---

# 31. Abel dismantles a laptop

This is where the system gets interesting.

Suppose:

```text
A23
HP EliteBook
```

contains:

```text
C50 Screen
C51 Motherboard
C52 RAM
C53 Battery
```

Abel lists the laptop as a whole.

Later he decides:

> "I want to sell the motherboard separately."

We don't create an entirely new physical object.

We already have:

```text
C51
```

So:

```text
C51
  ↓
Listing
```

Now the buyer can purchase the motherboard.

This is exactly why I think our architecture is heading in the right direction.

---

# 32. The important thing I DON'T want us to do yet

I don't want to immediately turn all of this into migrations.

Because there are still several questions the database needs to answer.

The most important ones are:

### A. Inventory architecture

Do we really want:

```text
inventory_items
```

to coexist with:

```text
assets
components
```

in exactly the way I've shown?

Or can we design a cleaner inventory abstraction?

---

### B. Listing ownership

Should the listing contain:

```text
asset_id
component_id
inventory_item_id
```

with two being null?

Or should we introduce a more elegant polymorphic:

```text
listable_type
listable_id
```

?

This is the question we previously discussed, and **now that we have the complete ecosystem in view, we can make that decision properly.**

---

### C. Multiple identical assets

Adam has:

```text
3 × HP EliteBook 840 G5
```

Do we show:

```text
HP EliteBook 840 G5
₦500,000
3 available
```

as **one listing**?

Or:

```text
Listing A → Asset 1
Listing B → Asset 2
Listing C → Asset 3
```

?

The UI prototype currently suggests grouping where appropriate, but the database needs to support the underlying physical assets.

---

### D. Component separation

We need to precisely model:

```text
Whole laptop listed
       ↓
Buyer buys motherboard
       ↓
Motherboard removed
       ↓
Remaining laptop
       ↓
New listings
```

That deserves a proper state-transition design.

---

### E. Order ownership

If a buyer buys:

```text
Laptop
+
Battery
+
RAM
```

from three different sellers, is that:

```text
3 Orders
```

or:

```text
1 Customer Order
    ├── Seller Order
    ├── Seller Order
    └── Seller Order
```

I strongly suspect the second architecture will eventually be better for Parts & Parcel.

---

### F. Money

We need to distinguish:

```text
Customer payment
Seller entitlement
Platform commission
Delivery fee
Refund
Seller payout
Subscription payment
```

rather than putting all of that into `orders`.

---

# 33. So our current conceptual model

At this point I'd summarize the database as:

```text
IDENTITY
────────────────
users
seller_profiles


CATALOG
────────────────
categories
brands
models
component_types
model_components


PHYSICAL INVENTORY
────────────────
assets
components
inventory_items


MARKETPLACE
────────────────
listings
offers
carts
cart_items
wishlists
wishlist_items


TRANSACTIONS
────────────────
orders
order_items
payments
shipments


COMMUNITY
────────────────
discussions
discussion_responses


PLATFORM / BILLING
────────────────
subscription_plans
subscriptions
usage_records
financial_transactions
```

That's the **current candidate architecture**, not the final schema.

And I think the next step should **not** be "write migrations."

The next step should be much more useful:

> **Take Adam, Abel and Seth and trace their actual data through these tables from registration → inventory → listing → browsing → offer → reservation → payment → order → fulfilment → sale → component separation → new listing.**

For each action, we'll literally show:

```text
USER ACTION
     ↓
TABLE
     ↓
ROW CREATED / UPDATED
     ↓
FIELD VALUES BEFORE
     ↓
FIELD VALUES AFTER
```

That will expose things like **whether inventory is redundant, whether `listing` should be polymorphic, exactly where reservation belongs, how component separation works, and whether our order model is sufficient** before we write a single migration.

---
